Yeah, I suppose you could say that. I mean, that's... that's all gone, too. \n\nI used to have things that settled me, doctor. You know? After a ten-hour shift on the concrete, dealing with the noise and the diesel fumes and the quotas... I had my ways of winding down. Fishing, for one. I used to love getting out by the reservoir, just the quiet of it. Taking my little aluminum boat out, casting a line, not even caring if I caught a single bluegill. It was just about the water. And choir practice—I used to sing bass at the community church down on 4th street. Every Wednesday night for practice, Sunday mornings for the service. It was good to see folks, to harmonize, to feel like you were part of something... normal, I guess. \n\nAnd my garage... I've got a whole setup in there. A lathe, a solid oak workbench, clamps, a drill press. I used to spend hours just tinkering with wood in the garage, turning bowls or making little planter boxes. The smell of the sawdust, the feel of the grain under a piece of fine sandpaper... it really used to settle my mind. It gave me peace.\n\nBut now? Now... I don't know. It's like someone just reached in and pulled the plug on all of it. \n\nI sit out in that garage now, like I was telling you, just hiding out in my car, trying to stay awake... and I can look right at my tools. I can look right at my fishing rods leaning up against the drywall in the corner, right next to my tackle box. And there is just... nothing. Absolutely no pull to touch them. I stare at the chisels and the wood glue, and it's like I'm looking at rocks on the moon. They don't mean anything to me anymore. I don't even have the urge to pick them up to dust them off. \n\nAnd as for seeing people... the choir director called a few times when I first stopped showing up, asking where I was, if I was sick. I just let it ring. I didn't pick up. I don't want to see them. I don't want to see anybody, really. The thought of standing up there in the robes, trying to smile and make small talk about the weather or the logistics business... it just feels completely impossible. I just don't have the interest. I don't have the spark for any of it. I look at the things I used to love, and it's all just dead weight now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your memory getting worse?\n\nPatient: Memory? Like... forgetting where I put my keys or... or not knowing what day it is? No. No, doctor, nothing like that. If anything, my memory is working overtime right now. It's almost too sharp, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nYou see, in my line of work, you absolutely cannot afford to have a bad memory. I've got an associate's degree, sure, but out on the warehouse floor, it's all about what you can hold in your head in real-time. We've got hundreds of thousands of square feet of inventory. I know exactly where the heavy-duty automotive parts are stored compared to the fragile electronics. I don't even need to look at the zone map anymore. I know that bay 47 has a slight dip in the concrete that you have to take at an angle so you don't tip a heavy load. I remember the exact weight limits for the different racking systems. You just... you absorb it, and you hold onto it. \n\nAnd lately, with everything going on... with the network and the dispatch system... I have to remember every single detail. I don't have a choice. I have to keep track of it all. I remember the exact sequence of the error codes that pop up on my scanner. Like last Thursday, it was error code 4-0-9-Delta, right at 2:14 PM. I didn't even have to write it down, it's just burned into my brain. I remember the license plates of the delivery trucks that idle too long near my specific loading dock. I remember the exact tone of the automated voice on the radio when it reroutes me. I track all of it in my head because I have to know what the system is doing. \n\nSo, no, my memory isn't slipping at all. I know exactly what I did yesterday, and I know exactly what my life was like twenty months ago before all this started. I remember how things used to be. Honestly, sometimes I wish my memory was getting worse. When I'm sitting out in my car in the garage, trying to stay awake, I remember how quiet my life used to be, how peaceful it was out on the reservoir... and it just makes it harder. But no, my brain is holding onto every single piece of information, every single detail, whether I want it to or not. No memory problems here.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech is hard to follow or that your thoughts seem jumbled?\n\nPatient: Jumbled? No. No, nobody has ever said anything like that to me. Not once. \n\nIf anything, I’m probably the only one making any actual sense down at the warehouse these days. You have to understand, doctor, when you are operating heavy machinery—when you're moving ten thousand pounds of automotive parts on a forklift—you cannot afford to have jumbled thoughts. And you certainly can't have jumbled speech. We use two-way radios to communicate with dispatch and the floor managers. It’s loud in there, you’ve got backup alarms beeping, diesel engines idling, the ventilation fans roaring... so when you key that mic, you have to be crystal clear. \n\nI use standard protocol. Clear, concise, exact. \"Bay 12 is clear, inbound freight is ready for unloading.\" \"Pallet jack needed at zone four.\" That sort of thing. I’ve never had a trucker tell me they couldn't understand my directions when I'm telling them which dock to back into. I’ve never had management tell me I'm not making sense when I report my daily totals. My thoughts are lined up perfectly straight, just like the inventory on the racks. \n\nNow, do they actually *listen* to me when I try to explain what the dispatch system is doing? When I try to show them how the network is targeting my specific scanner with those error codes? No. They brush me off. They tell me to just pull the battery out and put it back in, or they tell me to get back to work and stop worrying about the IT department's business. But that is absolutely not because my speech is hard to follow. They hear the words coming out of my mouth perfectly fine. They understand exactly what I'm saying. They just don't want to deal with it, or maybe they're part of why management is waiting for me to slip up, I don't know. \n\nBut my thoughts? My thoughts are sharp. Like I said before, I'm keeping track of every single detail, every code, every time the automated voice reroutes me. You can't do that if your brain is a mess. I can lay out everything that's been happening to me for the last twenty months, step by step, from the moment I clock in at 5:30 AM to the moment I pull out of that employee lot. I lay it all out perfectly straight. So no, doctor, no one has ever accused me of being jumbled. I know exactly what I'm saying.\n\nDoctor: Aside from avoiding people and hobbies, have you had trouble keeping up with basic daily tasks like bathing, dressing, cooking, or managing things at work?\n\nPatient: Trouble keeping up... yeah. Yeah, I suppose that’s... that’s the reality of it right now. \n\nIt’s funny you mention managing things at work, because... well, I’m not actually *at* work right now. Not on the floor, anyway. You see, doctor, there was... an incident. With the forklift. I was telling you earlier how I track everything, how I know the warehouse like the back of my hand, but... well, management didn't see things my way. After the incident, they actually pulled me into the front office—the main office with the glass windows overlooking the floor, not just the floor manager's little desk by the loading bays—and they took me off operating duties entirely. They put me on leave. They sat across from me and actually said I was no longer safe around the equipment. Me! After all my years navigating those narrow aisles without so much as clipping a rack. But they said I wasn't managing the floor safely anymore, so... they put me on leave. \n\nAnd at home... well, it’s not much better there, to be perfectly honest with you. My wife... she’s an absolute saint, she really is, but she has had to take over basically everything. I used to handle all our finances. I had a system, I kept track of the mortgage, the water bill, the insurance premiums... I used to balance the checkbook every Sunday evening. But lately? I just... I can't keep up with any of it reliably. I look at a stack of mail on the kitchen counter and it just feels like a mountain I can't climb. I can't process it. So she does it all now. \n\nShe pays the bills, she handles the bank accounts. She even has to manage my schedule—she’s the one who set up this appointment, actually, and she's the one who keeps track of when I'm supposed to be where, because I just can't keep it straight in my head to care about it. And the chores around the house... I used to mow the lawn every Saturday morning, edge the driveway, take care of the gutters in the fall. Now, I just sit out in the car in the garage. She's doing the cooking, the laundry, taking the trash bins down to the curb on Tuesday nights. I just... I can't seem to manage any of those basic things anymore. It’s like all my energy is just tied up in trying to stay awake and figure out what the system is doing, so she just... she has to do everything for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: See things? Like... like visions or shadows or something? No, no. My eyes work perfectly fine. I have to get my vision checked every single year to keep my operator certification current, 20/20 with my bifocals. I don't see anything that isn't physically right there in front of me on the concrete. \n\nBut... well, hearing things... that's a different situation entirely. That's what's really been keeping me on edge. \n\nI mentioned earlier that I spend a lot of time sitting out in my car in the garage these days. My wife, she probably thinks I'm just out there trying to get some peace and quiet, maybe listening to the sports radio or something. But I don't even have the key in the ignition. The engine is off. The garage door is pulled all the way down and locked. It's supposed to be dead silent in there. We live at the end of a cul-de-sac, doctor, so there isn't even any street traffic to make a sound. \n\nBut even then... even when I'm sitting in my garage with absolutely everything shut off... I hear them. I hear the dispatch voices. And it's not just a faint mumble through the walls, it is clear as day. It sounds exactly like the two-way radio is still clipped right there on my high-vis vest. I hear that little static click when someone keys the mic, and then I hear them calling my exact operator number. Four-two-seven-niner. They call my number, and they talk about me on the open channel. They say that I'm dangerous. They actually use that exact word, 'dangerous,' like I'm some kind of threat to the facility after all my years of clean safety records! \n\nAnd then... then they say that security is coming for me. I'll just be sitting there in the dark, gripping the steering wheel of my sedan, and I hear dispatch telling the floor managers that security is en route to apprehend me. I know I'm at home, I know I'm on leave and not on the clock, but I hear it so clearly\n\nDoctor: During these last twenty months, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... twenty months. It’s hard to even wrap my head around it being that long, but... yes. Yes, I have. Most of the day, nearly every single day, just like you said. It’s like dragging a ten-ton pallet of lead weights around with me everywhere I go. \n\nYou know, the thing is, there were times during all of this trouble—stretches where those warehouse fears I was telling you about, the dispatch voices and the panic about security—there were times when all of that actually quieted down for a little bit. Where my mind wasn't racing quite so fast about the automated system and I wasn't sitting in the car gripping the steering wheel quite so tight. But even then... even when that part of it gave me a bit of a breather... I was still just so incredibly down. Just... empty. Completely hollowed out inside, like someone had just pulled the plug and drained everything out of me. \n\nI used to be such an active guy on my weekends. I’ve got a beautiful bass boat sitting under a tarp next to the driveway, and I haven't even checked the battery on it in over a year. I have a full woodworking setup in the back half of the garage—table saw, drill press, a really nice lathe my wife got me for my fiftieth birthday. I used to spend hours out there turning these custom oak bowls, making things with my hands. But I just... I couldn't get myself back into any of it. Not the fishing, not the woodworking. Even when the warehouse stuff wasn't front and center, I couldn't even bring myself to sit down for family meals anymore. My wife would make a whole Sunday roast, set the dining room table, and I just couldn't sit there with them. I just felt too empty to participate in my own life. So, yes, doctor, it's been a constant heavy cloud this whole time, no matter what else was going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that specific question, because... yes. Yes, actually, there was a time like that. It’s hard to keep the timeline perfectly straight sometimes with everything going on, but I remember this one period clearly because it was just so... strange. 

There was a stretch—it lasted for several weeks, actually—when that heavy cloud, that ten-ton pallet of lead weights I was telling you about, it actually lifted for a bit. My mood had eased some. I wasn't feeling that crushing emptiness every single minute of the day. I was actually coming out of the garage, coming back into the house, and sitting down at the dining room table again. My wife had made her pot roast, the one with the little red potatoes and the thick gravy, and I actually ate a full plate. I was eating, and I was talking a little more, too. Just about normal things, you know? The weather, the neighbor's dog, the price of gas. It felt... well, it felt like I could finally take a breath. 

But the thing is, doctor... the warehouse never left me alone. Not even then. 

Even during those several weeks when I was feeling better, when I wasn't depressed and I was eating and talking with my wife... I still heard them. I'd be sitting right there at the dinner table, chewing my food, and I would hear the static click. I still heard dispatch calling my number. Four-two-seven-niner. Clear as a bell, right in my ear, talking about how dangerous I was. And on top of that, I still believed the routing system was tracking me. You know the automated routing system we use for the pallets? The one that scans the barcodes and directs the forklifts through the aisles? I knew, without a shadow of a doubt, that it was tracking my movements right there in my own house. 

It’s just... it's so hard to explain to someone who hasn't lived it. How can you feel alright, you know, relatively speaking—how can your mood be fine, but you're still completely convinced that an industrial warehouse computer is mapping your kitchen? But that's exactly what was happening for those weeks. I'd just sit there, having a totally normal conversation with my wife, passing the salt, all while knowing the routing system had locked onto my coordinates and dispatch was monitoring my every move. So, yes, the mood got better for a few weeks, but the rest of it... the rest of it didn't stop at all.

My wife, she probably thinks I'm just out there trying to get some peace and quiet, maybe listening to the sports radio or something. But I don't even have the key in the ignition. The engine is off. The garage door is pulled all the way down and locked. It's supposed to be dead silent in there. We live at the end of a cul-de-sac, doctor, so there isn't even any street traffic to make a sound. \n\nBut even then... even when I'm sitting in my garage with absolutely everything shut off... I hear them. I hear the dispatch voices. And it's not just a faint mumble through the walls, it is clear as day. It sounds exactly like the two-way radio is still clipped right there on my high-vis vest. I hear that little static click when someone keys the mic, and then I hear them calling my exact operator number. Four-two-seven-niner. They call my number, and they talk about me on the open channel. They say that I'm dangerous. They actually use that exact word, 'dangerous,' like I'm some kind of threat to the facility after all my years of clean safety records! \n\nAnd then... then they say that security is coming for me. I'll just be sitting there in the dark, gripping the steering wheel of my sedan, and I hear dispatch telling the floor managers that security is en route to apprehend me. I know I'm at home, I know I'm on leave and not on the clock, but I hear it so clearly\n\nDoctor: During these last twenty months, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... twenty months. It’s hard to even wrap my head around it being that long, but... yes. Yes, I have. Most of the day, nearly every single day, just like you said. It’s like dragging a ten-ton pallet of lead weights around with me everywhere I go. \n\nYou know, the thing is, there were times during all of this trouble—stretches where those warehouse fears I was telling you about, the dispatch voices and the panic about security—there were times when all of that actually quieted down for a little bit. Where my mind wasn't racing quite so fast about the automated system and I wasn't sitting in the car gripping the steering wheel quite so tight. But even then... even when that part of it gave me a bit of a breather... I was still just so incredibly down. Just... empty. Completely hollowed out inside, like someone had just pulled the plug and drained everything out of me. \n\nI used to be such an active guy on my weekends. I’ve got a beautiful bass boat sitting under a tarp next to the driveway, and I haven't even checked the battery on it in over a year. I have a full woodworking setup in the back half of the garage—table saw, drill press, a really nice lathe my wife got me for my fiftieth birthday. I used to spend hours out there turning these custom oak bowls, making things with my hands. But I just... I couldn't get myself back into any of it. Not the fishing, not the woodworking. Even when the warehouse stuff wasn't front and center, I couldn't even bring myself to sit down for family meals anymore. My wife would make a whole Sunday roast, set the dining room table, and I just couldn't sit there with them. I just felt too empty to participate in my own life. So, yes, doctor, it's been a constant heavy cloud this whole time, no matter what else was going on.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the dispatch voices or believed the warehouse system was targeting you, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that specific question, because... yes. Yes, actually, there was a time like that. It’s hard to keep the timeline perfectly straight sometimes with everything going on, but I remember this one period clearly because it was just so... strange. \n\nThere was a stretch—it lasted for several weeks, actually—when that heavy cloud, that ten-ton pallet of lead weights I was telling you about, it actually lifted for a bit. My mood had eased some. I wasn't feeling that crushing emptiness every single minute of the day. I was actually coming out of the garage, coming back into the house, and sitting down at the dining room table again. My wife had made her pot roast, the one with the little red potatoes and the thick gravy, and I actually ate a full plate. I was eating, and I was talking a little more, too. Just about normal things, you know? The weather, the neighbor's dog, the price of gas. It felt... well, it felt like I could finally take a breath. \n\nBut the thing is, doctor... the warehouse never left me alone. Not even then. \n\nEven during those several weeks when I was feeling better, when I wasn't depressed and I was eating and talking with my wife... I still heard them. I'd be sitting right there at the dinner table, chewing my food, and I would hear the static click. I still heard dispatch calling my number. Four-two-seven-niner. Clear as a bell, right in my ear, talking about how dangerous I was. And on top of that, I still believed the routing system was tracking me. You know the automated routing system we use for the pallets? The one that scans the barcodes and directs the forklifts through the aisles? I knew, without a shadow of a doubt, that it was tracking my movements right there in my own house. \n\nIt’s just... it's so hard to explain to someone who hasn't lived it. How can you feel alright, you know, relatively speaking—how can your mood be fine, but you're still completely convinced that an industrial warehouse computer is mapping your kitchen? But that's exactly what was happening for those weeks. I'd just sit there, having a totally normal conversation with my wife, passing the salt, all while knowing the routing system had locked onto my coordinates and dispatch was monitoring my every move. So, yes, the mood got better for a few weeks, but the rest of it... the rest of it didn't stop at all."
